Discretized into bins:\n  - 0: < 2 cm/s\n  - 1: 2-10 cm/s\n  - 2: 10-20 cm/s\n  - 3: 20-40 cm/s\n  - 4: > 40 cm/s\n- Lick, time-varying. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n- Reward zone location, per-trial. 0 = A, 1 = B, 2 = C\n- Reward outcome, per-trial. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. false}\n================================================================================\nSTEP 9 [agent]\nMSG: Executed Read toolu_013Xco8Bxg6LSSd2bZqXEh12\nTOOL: Read ARGS: {\"file_path\": \"/app/methods.txt\"}\nOBS: 1→# Overview\n     2→\n     3→To dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30–40 min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean ± s.d., 5.8 ± 1.9 days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\n    10→\n    11→## Hidden reward zone task\n    12→\n    13→The main ‘switch’ task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450 cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a ‘hidden’, unmarked 50 cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a ‘teleport period’ that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (‘jitter period’) between 1 and 5 s (5–10 s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50 cm giving the appearance of a gray ‘tunnel,’ before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0 cm.\n    14→\n    15→Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n = 11 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n = 9 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days 1–2 of the task. On day 3 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). The new reward zone was maintained on day 4. On day 5 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day 6 and moved back to the original location on day 7 (for example, A). Discretization bins:\n  - 0:  < -50 cm\n  - 1: -50 to -10 cm\n  - 2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\n  - 3: 0 cm\n  - 4: >0 cm to +10 cm\n  - 5: +10 to +50 cm\n  - 6: > +50 cm\n- Absolute position in corridor, time-varying. Discretized into 5 equal-sized bins spanning the 450 cm track:\n  - 0: < 90 cm\n  - 1: 90 to 180 cm\n  - 2: 180 to 270 cm\n  - 3: 270 to 360 cm\n  - 4: > 360 cm\n- Speed, time-varying. Discretized into bins:\n  - 0: < 2 cm/s\n  - 1: 2-10 cm/s\n  - 2: 10-20 cm/s\n  - 3: 20-40 cm/s\n  - 4: > 40 cm/s\n- Lick, time-varying. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n- Reward zone location, per-trial. 0 = A, 1 = B, 2 = C\n- Reward outcome, per-trial. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. \"/app/methods.txt\"}\nOBS: 1→# Overview\n     2→\n     3→To dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. Option `keep_teleports=True` extends windows to include teleport periods.\\n- **Bleedthrough correction**: If a second imaging channel exists, linear regression removes bleedthrough from the other channel's signal per cell.\\n- **Neuropil correction**: `neuropil_method='subtract'` subtracts `neu_coef * f_neu` (default 0.7). After subtraction, the neuropil mean per trial is added back to avoid dividing by small numbers.\\n- **Baseline (`flow`)**: `'maximin'` method applies smoothing (sigma=15), then minimum filter (window=300 samples, ~20 sec), then maximum filter (same window) -- a morphological opening operation.\\n- **dF/F**: `(F - baseline) / |baseline|`, then Gaussian-smoothed by 2 time bins per trial.\\n- **Deconvolution**: Optional OASIS deconvolution from suite2p (`dcnv.oasis`) with configurable `tau` (default 0.7) and frame rate per plane. Returns `spks` alongside `dff`.\\n\\n**Key variables:**\\n- `f_` -- trial-masked fluorescence; `flow` -- baseline; `dff` -- delta-F/F (ROIs x time); `spks` -- deconvolved events.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 2. dayData.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/dayData.py`)\\n\\n**The `dayData` class** is the central data structure holding one experiment day's data across multiple animals.\\n\\n**Initialization parameters (with defaults):**\\n- `place_cell_logical = 'or'` -- cells classified as place cells if significant SI in set 0 OR set 1.\\n- `ts_key = 'dff'` -- timeseries used for place cell peak finding.\\n- `speed_thr = 2` cm/s -- running speed threshold for spatial binning.\\n- `int_thresh = 0.5` / `int_method = 'speed'` -- putative interneuron detection via speed correlation.\\n- `reward_dist_exclusive = 50` cm / `reward_dist_inclusive = 50` cm -- distance thresholds for reward-cell and reward-relative classifications.\\n- `bin_size = 10` cm -- spatial bin size. `sigma = 1` bin -- Gaussian smoothing.\\n- `lick_correction_thr = 0.35` -- threshold for detecting stuck lick sensors.\\n\\n**Construction flow (per animal):**\\n1. `add_trial_dict_info()` -- extracts trial subsets (set 0 / set 1), reward zone positions and labels per trial set.\\n2. Stores `activity_matrix[an]` (trials x bins x cells) and `events[an]`.\\n4. `add_spatial_peaks()` -- finds argmax of mean activity per trial set per cell.\\n5. `filter_cell_subset()` -- applies OR/AND logic, excludes putative interneurons, identifies end cells (peak at first/last bin), reward cells (peaks near both reward zones), and track cells (peaks stable within 50 cm, not near reward).\\n\\n**`add_all_the_things()` adds:**\\n- 10-trial blocks per trial set.\\n- Population metrics: PV similarity matrices (correlation or cosine), place cell peak distributions, reward fraction per block, rate maps.\\n- Lick metrics: current-zone lick rate, other-zone lick rate, current-vs-other ratio, in-vs-out ratio, lick position std and COM.\\n- Cell class masks (track-relative, appear, disappear, reward, nonreward-remap).\\n- Circular reward-relative peaks with null distribution (1000 permutations), cross-correlation verification.\\n\\n**Key stored attributes per animal:**\\n- `activity_matrix[an]` -- trials x position bins x neurons.\\n- `peaks[an]['set 0']` / `['set 1']` -- peak position per cell per trial set.\\n- `overall_place_cell_masks[an]` -- boolean, which cells are place cells (OR logic, excluding interneurons).\\n- `sim_mat[an]` -- trial-by-trial correlation matrices for `'pv'`, `'lick'`, `'speed'`.\\n- `rel_peaks[an]['set 0']` / `['set 1']` -- peaks in circular coordinates relative to reward zone start.\\n- `reward_rel_cell_ids[an]` -- integer indices of confirmed reward-relative cells.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 3. sessions_dict.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/sessions_dict.py`)\\n\\n**Pure metadata**: Two dictionaries (`single_plane`, `multi_plane`) mapping animal names to tuples of session dictionaries.\\n\\n**Per session entry:**\\n- `date` -- dd_mm_yyyy format.\\n- `scene` -- VR environment+location label (e.g., `'Env1_LocationA'`, `'Env\n================================================================================\nSTEP 20 [agent]\nREASONING: Excellent! There are no NWB files in this codebase -- the data originates from ScanBox (.sbx) imaging files and Unity VR SQLite databases, processed through suite2p and the `TwoPUtils` library. Let me now provide the comprehensive summary.\\n\\n---\\n\\nHere is a detailed summary of each file's role in the data loading and processing pipeline:\\n\\n## 1. preprocessing.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py`)\\n\\n**Data loading (not NWB -- uses ScanBox + SQLite):**\\n- Raw imaging data comes from **ScanBox `.sbx` files** (two-photon calcium imaging) and **Unity VR `.sqlite` files** (behavioral data from virtual reality).\\n- `create_sess()` builds a `TwoPUtils.sess.Session` object by specifying paths to scan files, VR files, and suite2p output directories.\\n- `append_session_data()` sequentially loads scan info, aligns VR data to 2P frame times (`sess.align_VR_to_2P()`), loads suite2p ROI data, and adds behavioral timeseries (licks, rewards, speed).\\n\\n**VR alignment (`vr_align_to_mock_2P`):**\\n- Downsamples VR data to the 2P frame rate using interpolation.\\n- **Linear interpolation** for position (`pos`) and time parameter (`t`).\\n- **Nearest-frame interpolation** for categorical columns (`morph`, `trialnum`, `cmd`, etc.).\\n- **Cumulative-sum then diff** for event columns (`lick`, `reward`, `teleport`, `tstart`, `rzone`, `dz`) to avoid losing counts during resampling.\\n- Speed is computed from Gaussian-smoothed cumulative `dz` (displacement), divided by frame time.\\n- Lick rate is lick count / frame time, then Gaussian-smoothed (sigma=5).\\n\\n**dF/F calculation (`dff()`):**\\n- Input: `f` (ROI fluorescence, shape ROIs x samples), `f_neu` (neuropil), trial start/teleport indices.\\n- **NaN-masking**: Only data within trial boundaries (start to teleport) is kept; inter-trial intervals become NaN. Option `keep_teleports=True` extends windows to include teleport periods.\\n- **Bleedthrough correction**: If a second imaging channel exists, linear regression removes bleedthrough from the other channel's signal per cell.\\n- **Neuropil correction**: `neuropil_method='subtract'` subtracts `neu_coef * f_neu` (default 0.7). After subtraction, the neuropil mean per trial is added back to avoid dividing by small numbers.\\n- **Baseline (`flow`)**: `'maximin'` method applies smoothing (sigma=15), then minimum filter (window=300 samples, ~20 sec), then maximum filter (same window) -- a morphological opening operation.\\n- **dF/F**: `(F - baseline) / |baseline|`, then Gaussian-smoothed by 2 time bins per trial.\\n- **Deconvolution**: Optional OASIS deconvolution from suite2p (`dcnv.oasis`) with configurable `tau` (default 0.7) and frame rate per plane. Returns `spks` alongside `dff`.\\n\\n**Key variables:**\\n- `f_` -- trial-masked fluorescence; `flow` -- baseline; `dff` -- delta-F/F (ROIs x time); `spks` -- deconvolved events.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 2. dayData.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/dayData.py`)\\n\\n**The `dayData` class** is the central data structure holding one experiment day's data across multiple animals.\\n\\n**Initialization parameters (with defaults):**\\n- `place_cell_logical = 'or'` -- cells classified as place cells if significant SI in set 0 OR set 1.\\n- `ts_key = 'dff'` -- timeseries used for place cell peak finding.\\n- `speed_thr = 2` cm/s -- running speed threshold for spatial binning.\\n- `int_thresh = 0.5` / `int_method = 'speed'` -- putative interneuron detection via speed correlation.\\n- `reward_dist_exclusive = 50` cm / `reward_dist_inclusive = 50` cm -- distance thresholds for reward-cell and reward-relative classifications.\\n- `bin_size = 10` cm -- spatial bin size. `sigma = 1` bin -- Gaussian smoothing.\\n- `lick_correction_thr = 0.35` -- threshold for detecting stuck lick sensors.\\n\\n**Construction flow (per animal):**\\n1. `add_trial_dict_info()` -- extracts trial subsets (set 0 / set 1), reward zone positions and labels per trial set.\\n2. Copies place cell masks and SI from the pre-computed `multi_an_sess` dictionary.\\n3. `add_activity_matrix()` -- recomputes spatially-binned trial matrix (`TwoPUtils.spatial_analyses.trial_matrix`) with speed threshold applied. Stores `activity_matrix[an]` (trials x bins x cells) and `events[an]`.\\n4. `add_spatial_peaks()` -- finds argmax of mean activity per trial set per cell.\\n5. `filter_cell_subset()` -- applies OR/AND logic, excludes putative interneurons, identifies end cells (peak at first/last bin), reward cells (peaks near both reward zones), and track cells (peaks stable within 50 cm, not near reward).\\n\\n**`add_all_the_things()` adds:**\\n- 10-trial blocks per trial set.\\n- Population metrics: PV similarity matrices (correlation or cosine), place cell peak distributions, reward fraction per block, rate maps.\\n- Lick metrics: current-zone lick rate, other-zone lick rate, current-vs-other ratio, in-vs-out ratio, lick position std and COM.\\n- Cell class masks (track-relative, appear, disappear, reward, nonreward-remap).\\n- Circular reward-relative peaks with null distribution (1000 permutations), cross-correlation verification.\\n\\n**Key stored attributes per animal:**\\n- `activity_matrix[an]` -- trials x position bins x neurons.\\n- `peaks[an]['set 0']` / `['set 1']` -- peak position per cell per trial set.\\n- `overall_place_cell_masks[an]` -- boolean, which cells are place cells (OR logic, excluding interneurons).\\n- `sim_mat[an]` -- trial-by-trial correlation matrices for `'pv'`, `'lick'`, `'speed'`.\\n- `rel_peaks[an]['set 0']` / `['set 1']` -- peaks in circular coordinates relative to reward zone start.\\n- `reward_rel_cell_ids[an]` -- integer indices of confirmed reward-relative cells.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 3. sessions_dict.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/sessions_dict.py`)\\n\\n**Pure metadata**: Two dictionaries (`single_plane`, `multi_plane`) mapping animal names to tuples of session dictionaries.\\n\\n**Per session entry:**\\n- `date` -- dd_mm_yyyy format.\\n- `scene` -- VR environment+location label (e.g., `'Env1_LocationA'`, `'Env\n================================================================================\nSTEP 20 [agent]\nREASONING: Excellent! VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n321:     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30–40 min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean ± s.d., 5.8 ± 1.9 days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\n325:    13→The main ‘switch’ task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450 cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a ‘hidden’, unmarked 50 cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a ‘teleport period’ that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (‘jitter period’) between 1 and 5 s (5–10 s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50 cm giving the appearance of a gray ‘tunnel,’ before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0 cm.\n327:    15→Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n = 11 mice). On day 3 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). The new reward zone was maintained on day 4. There are no NWB files in this codebase -- the data originates from ScanBox (.sbx) imaging files and Unity VR SQLite databases, processed through suite2p and the `TwoPUtils` library. Let me now provide the comprehensive summary.\\n\\n---\\n\\nHere is a detailed summary of each file's role in the data loading and processing pipeline:\\n\\n## 1. preprocessing.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py`)\\n\\n**Data loading (not NWB -- uses ScanBox + SQLite):**\\n- Raw imaging data comes from **ScanBox `.sbx` files** (two-photon calcium imaging) and **Unity VR `.sqlite` files** (behavioral data from virtual reality).\\n- `create_sess()` builds a `TwoPUtils.sess.Session` object by specifying paths to scan files, VR files, and suite2p output directories.\\n- `append_session_data()` sequentially loads scan info, aligns VR data to 2P frame times (`sess.align_VR_to_2P()`), loads suite2p ROI data, and adds behavioral timeseries (licks, rewards, speed).\\n\\n**VR alignment (`vr_align_to_mock_2P`):**\\n- Downsamples VR data to the 2P frame rate using interpolation.\\n- **Linear interpolation** for position (`pos`) and time parameter (`t`).\\n- **Nearest-frame interpolation** for categorical columns (`morph`, `trialnum`, `cmd`, etc.).\\n- **Cumulative-sum then diff** for event columns (`lick`, `reward`, `teleport`, `tstart`, `rzone`, `dz`) to avoid losing counts during resampling.\\n- Speed is computed from Gaussian-smoothed cumulative `dz` (displacement), divided by frame time.\\n- Lick rate is lick count / frame time, then Gaussian-smoothed (sigma=5).\\n\\n**dF/F calculation (`dff()`):**\\n- Input: `f` (ROI fluorescence, shape ROIs x samples), `f_neu` (neuropil), trial start/teleport indices.\\n- **NaN-masking**: Only data within trial boundaries (start to teleport) is kept; inter-trial intervals become NaN. Option `keep_teleports=True` extends windows to include teleport periods.\\n- **Bleedthrough correction**: If a second imaging channel exists, linear regression removes bleedthrough from the other channel's signal per cell.\\n- **Neuropil correction**: `neuropil_method='subtract'` subtracts `neu_coef * f_neu` (default 0.7). After subtraction, the neuropil mean per trial is added back to avoid dividing by small numbers.\\n- **Baseline (`flow`)**: `'maximin'` method applies smoothing (sigma=15), then minimum filter (window=300 samples, ~20 sec), then maximum filter (same window) -- a morphological opening operation.\\n- **dF/F**: `(F - baseline) / |baseline|`, then Gaussian-smoothed by 2 time bins per trial.\\n- **Deconvolution**: Optional OASIS deconvolution from suite2p (`dcnv.oasis`) with configurable `tau` (default 0.7) and frame rate per plane. Returns `spks` alongside `dff`.\\n\\n**Key variables:**\\n- `f_` -- trial-masked fluorescence; `flow` -- baseline; `dff` -- delta-F/F (ROIs x time); `spks` -- deconvolved events.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 2. dayData.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/dayData.py`)\\n\\n**The `dayData` class** is the central data structure holding one experiment day's data across multiple animals.\\n\\n**Initialization parameters (with defaults):**\\n- `place_cell_logical = 'or'` -- cells classified as place cells if significant SI in set 0 OR set 1.\\n- `ts_key = 'dff'` -- timeseries used for place cell peak finding.\\n- `speed_thr = 2` cm/s -- running speed threshold for spatial binning.\\n- `int_thresh = 0.5` / `int_method = 'speed'` -- putative interneuron detection via speed correlation.\\n- `reward_dist_exclusive = 50` cm / `reward_dist_inclusive = 50` cm -- distance thresholds for reward-cell and reward-relative classifications.\\n- `bin_size = 10` cm -- spatial bin size. `sigma = 1` bin -- Gaussian smoothing.\\n- `lick_correction_thr = 0.35` -- threshold for detecting stuck lick sensors.\\n\\n**Construction flow (per animal):**\\n1. `add_trial_dict_info()` -- extracts trial subsets (set 0 / set 1), reward zone positions and labels per trial set.\\n2. Copies place cell masks and SI from the pre-computed `multi_an_sess` dictionary.\\n3. `add_activity_matrix()` -- recomputes spatially-binned trial matrix (`TwoPUtils.spatial_analyses.trial_matrix`) with speed threshold applied. Stores `activity_matrix[an]` (trials x bins x cells) and `events[an]`.\\n4. `add_spatial_peaks()` -- finds argmax of mean activity per trial set per cell.\\n5. `filter_cell_subset()` -- applies OR/AND logic, excludes putative interneurons, identifies end cells (peak at first/last bin), reward cells (peaks near both reward zones), and track cells (peaks stable within 50 cm, not near reward).\\n\\n**`add_all_the_things()` adds:**\\n- 10-trial blocks per trial set.\\n- Population metrics: PV similarity matrices (correlation or cosine), place cell peak distributions, reward fraction per block, rate maps.\\n- Lick metrics: current-zone lick rate, other-zone lick rate, current-vs-other ratio, in-vs-out ratio, lick position std and COM.\\n- Cell class masks (track-relative, appear, disappear, reward, nonreward-remap).\\n- Circular reward-relative peaks with null distribution (1000 permutations), cross-correlation verification.\\n\\n**Key stored attributes per animal:**\\n- `activity_matrix[an]` -- trials x position bins x neurons.\\n- `peaks[an]['set 0']` / `['set 1']` -- peak position per cell per trial set.\\n- `overall_place_cell_masks[an]` -- boolean, which cells are place cells (OR logic, excluding interneurons).\\n- `sim_mat[an]` -- trial-by-trial correlation matrices for `'pv'`, `'lick'`, `'speed'`.\\n- `rel_peaks[an]['set 0']` / `['set 1']` -- peaks in circular coordinates relative to reward zone start.\\n- `reward_rel_cell_ids[an]` -- integer indices of confirmed reward-relative cells.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 3. sessions_dict.py (`/app/code/src/reward_relative/sessions_dict.py`)\\n\\n**Pure metadata**: Two dictionaries (`single_plane`, `multi_plane`) mapping animal names to tuples of session dictionaries.\\n\\n**Per session entry:**\\n- `date` -- dd_mm_yyyy format.\\n- `scene` -- VR environment+location label (e.g., `'Env1_LocationA'`, `'Env\n637:1.
